<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We disprove Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture by constructing a finite field <i>K</i> of characteristic two, a finitely presented group <i>G</i>, and finite sums $`a,b\in K[G]`$ with $`ab=1`$ but $`ba\ne1`$. The group <i>G</i> is nonsofic. The same elements define a cellular automaton on <i>K</i><sup><i>G</i></sup> that is injective but not surjective, disproving Gottschalk's surjunctivity conjecture.

- **claim-main-counterexample** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exist a finite field $K$ of characteristic two, a finitely presented group $G$ containing an element of odd prime order, and elements $a_{\mathrm{out}},b_{\mathrm{out}}\in K[G]$ such that $$a_{\mathrm{out}}b_{\mathrm{out}}=1, \qquad b_{\mathrm{out}}a_{\mathrm{out}}\ne1.$$ The field, group, and finite sums are specified by a terminating prescription involving finite sets and finite-field arithmetic.
  - Explanation: Gives an explicit one-sided invertible pair of finite sums in a group algebra over a finite field of characteristic two. The group is finitely presented and has an element of odd prime order. The reverse product is not the identity, so Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture fails.
- **claim-interpolation-simplex** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-interpolation)): Let $Q$ be a field of order $q$, enumerate its elements as $a_0,\ldots,a_{q-1}$, and let $0\le d<q$. For $n\ge1$, set $$S_d=\{(a_{k_1},\ldots,a_{k_n}): k_i\ge0,\ \textstyle\sum_i k_i\le d\}\subset Q^n.$$ Evaluation on $S_d$ is an isomorphism from the $Q$-vector space of polynomials of total degree at most $d$ to $Q^{S_d}$.
  - Explanation: Evaluation on the index simplex determines a polynomial of bounded total degree uniquely and realizes every prescribed value pattern. This supplies indicator polynomials for the chosen hole points.
- **claim-line-sums-vanish** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-line-sums)): Let $n\ge1$. If $P$ is a polynomial on $Q^n$ of total degree at most $q-2$, then its sum on every full affine $Q$-line is zero. Its sum on $Q^n$ is also zero.
  - Explanation: A polynomial of total degree at most two less than the field order sums to zero on every full affine line and on the whole space. The proof uses a character-sum argument over the multiplicative group of the field.
- **claim-pairing-by-holes** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-pairing)): Let $q\ge4$ be a power of two, let $Q$ be a field of order $q$, fix an enumeration of $Q$, and let $n\ge1$. Put $d_0=(q-2)/2$, $S=S_{d_0}\subset\mathcal A=Q^n$, and $N_{\rm pt}=q^n$. Let $\ell$ be an odd prime and $D=\ell+1$. Suppose that $O\subset S$ and that $\mathcal F$ is a family of full affine $Q$-lines contained in $V=\mathcal A\setminus O$, with $$\label{eq:hole-size} \frac{2N_{\rm pt}}{D^2}\le \|O\|\le\frac{7N_{\rm pt}}{D^2}.$$ Suppose also that every point of $V$ lies on $D$ members of $\mathcal F$ and that the incidence graph has girth at least $12$. If $K$ is a finite extension of $Q$ containing a primitive $\ell$th root of unity, then, with $m=\|O\|$ and $t=\|V\|$, there are columns $x_v\in K^m$, $v\in V$, such that $$0<m<t\le m\ell^2,\qquad \sum_{v\in V}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=-I_m,\qquad \sum_{v\in f}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=0\quad(f\in\mathcal F).$$
  - Explanation: From a regular family of full lines of large girth that avoid a set of holes, builds vectors whose rank-one pairings sum to minus the identity overall and to zero on each line. The size inequalities needed for packing follow from the hole-count bounds.
- **claim-incidence-criterion** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-criterion)): Let $\ell$ be an odd prime, let $K$ be a finite field of characteristic two containing a primitive $\ell$th root of unity $\zeta$, and put $\kappa=\ell^2$. Let $V$ be a finite set of cardinality $t$, and let $\mathcal F$ be a finite family of nonempty subsets of $V$. Assume that every $v\in V$ belongs to exactly $\ell+1$ members of $\mathcal F$ and that the bipartite incidence graph of $V$ and $\mathcal F$ has girth at least $12$.* *Suppose that an integer $m$ and column vectors $x_v\in K^m$, $v\in V$, satisfy $$\label{eq:criterion-size} 0<m<t\le m\kappa$$ and $$\label{eq:criterion-pairing} \sum_{v\in V}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=-I_m, \qquad \sum_{v\in f}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=0 \quad\text{for every }f\in\mathcal F.$$ Then there are a finitely presented group $G$ containing an element of order $\ell$ and finite sums $a_{\mathrm{out}},b_{\mathrm{out}}\in K[G]$ such that $$a_{\mathrm{out}}b_{\mathrm{out}}=1, \qquad b_{\mathrm{out}}a_{\mathrm{out}}\ne1.$$ They are given by the constructions and formulas below.
  - Explanation: Turns regular high-girth incidence data together with vectors satisfying two matrix identities into a finitely presented group and finite sums with forward product one and reverse product nonidentity. The proof glues local groups, packs by character idempotents, and uses matrix units from an auxiliary finite group.
- **claim-local-groups-embed** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-embedding)): If the incidence graph of $(V,\mathcal F)$ has girth at least $12$, then every homomorphism $\theta_v:K_v^0\to N$ is injective.
  - Explanation: Under the girth hypothesis, each local copy of the additive plane group embeds in the glued group. A collapsed generator would give a planar diagram contradicting Euler's formula.
- **claim-hypergraph-existence** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-hypergraph)): For all sufficiently large $h$, for every $Q$ and enumeration as above, there are a set $O\subset S$ and a set $\mathcal F$ of affine lines contained in $V=\mathcal A\setminus O$ such that $$\label{eq:hypergraph-hole-size} \frac{2N_{\rm pt}}{D^2}\le \|O\|\le\frac{7N_{\rm pt}}{D^2},$$ every point of $V$ lies on exactly $D$ members of $\mathcal F$, and the bipartite incidence graph of $V$ and $\mathcal F$ has girth at least $12$.
  - Explanation: For all large field parameters there is a hole set of controlled size and a family of full affine lines avoiding it. Every remaining point has exactly the prescribed degree and the incidence graph has the required girth.
- **claim-direction-spaces** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-direction-spaces)): There are two-dimensional subspaces $W_r\subset\mathcal A$, one per bundle, and $(b-1)$-dimensional subspaces $E_j\subset\mathcal A$, one per index, such that any at most ten distinct members of this list have direct sum. Choose a direction $e_r$ in each $W_r$, and assign its other $q$ directions bijectively to the indices in that bundle, calling them $d_j$. Then $U_j=d_j\oplus E_j$ has dimension $b$, and $$\label{eq:hypergraph-intersections} U_j\cap U_{j'}=0\quad(j\ne j'),\qquad U_j\cap e_r=0,\qquad U_j\cap W_r= \begin{cases}d_j,&r=\rho(j),\\0,&r\ne\rho(j). \end{cases}$$
  - Explanation: Produces two-dimensional bundle spaces and higher-dimensional index spaces that are independent in groups of at most ten, via a Vandermonde argument over an extension field. The resulting intersection pattern separates generic directions from replacement directions.
- **claim-compatible-holes** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-holes)): For all sufficiently large $h$, there is $O\subset S$ satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:hypergraph-hole-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hypergraph-hole-size">[eq:hypergraph-hole-size]</a> and the following properties.* 1. *Every translate of every $W_r$ contains at most one point of $O$.* 2. *For every $j$, every coset $C$ of $U_j$, and every $r$, $\|O\cap(C+W_r)\|\le100$.* 3. *No exceptional rectangle has four distinct witnesses in $O$, one for each of its four side lines.
  - Explanation: A random selection followed by deletion of marked points yields holes that are sparse in every replacement plane, bounded in every relevant coset, and free of exceptional rectangle witnesses.
- **claim-replacement-lines** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-replacement-lines)): The replacement lines avoid $O$ and are pairwise disjoint within each label $R_r$. No exceptional rectangle has all four side lines among these replacement lines.
  - Explanation: Replacement lines miss every hole, are disjoint within a label, and cannot form a rectangle by themselves, because the hole witnesses would have to be four distinct points.
- **claim-polygon-classification** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-polygons)): Every candidate sequence with a generic step has one of the following forms.* 1. *A rectangle alternating $G_j,R_r$, with opposite vectors paired.* 2. *A rectangle alternating distinct $G_j,G_{j'}$, with opposite vectors paired.* 3. *A pentagon containing two $G_j$ steps, two $G_{j'}$ steps, and one $R_r$ step, where $\rho(j)=\rho(j')=r$.* *If $g$ is the number of distinct generic labels, its bad event uses $2g$ distinct random variables and has probability $p_*^{2g}$. In case <span class="upright">(iii)</span>, the exceptional vector and one vector of each generic label determine the other two vectors, once their order is fixed.
  - Explanation: Every short closed candidate sequence containing a generic step is one of three rigid shapes, and each corresponding bad event depends on a bounded number of independent variables with a product probability.
- **claim-event-sum-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-event-bounds)): Let $\mu$ be the largest, over all variables $P_{j,C}$, of the sum of probabilities of bad events using that variable. Then $\mu=O(q^{-7})$, uniformly in the variable, and every event uses at most four variables.
  - Explanation: The total probability of bad events touching any one slab variable is small uniformly in the variable, and each event involves at most four variables.
- **claim-local-lemma-variant** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-local-lemma)): Let a finite collection of events be determined by independent random variables, with each event assigned a nonempty set of at most four variables that determine it. If the sum of event probabilities at every variable is at most $1/16$, there is positive probability that no event occurs.
  - Explanation: A product-form local lemma: if each event is determined by few independent variables and the probability mass at each variable is small, then with positive probability no event occurs. The proof is by conditional probabilities and needs no resampling.
- **claim-slab-line-partition** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-slab-partition)): Let a three-dimensional affine space have direction $P\oplus d$, where $\dim_QP=2$ and $\dim_Qd=1$. If it has no designated point, it has a line partition whose directions project nontrivially to $P$. If it has a designated point $o$, it has a line partition containing $o+d$, with every other line’s direction projecting nontrivially to $P$.
  - Explanation: Gives explicit line partitions of a three-dimensional slab, optionally containing a designated axis through a hole, using an operator without eigenvectors on the plane part.
- **claim-local-trade-degrees** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-trade)): For every hole $o$ and bundle $r$, remove the $q$ lines $o+d_j$ with $\rho(j)=r$, and insert the $q-1$ replacement lines of label $R_r$ in $o+W_r$. After all these operations, the resulting set $\mathcal F$ consists of lines in $V$, and every point of $V$ has degree $D$. The surviving lines of each preliminary partition are pairwise disjoint, as are the replacement lines within each label. There are no duplicate lines across different labels.
  - Explanation: Swapping the fan of axes through each hole for parallel replacement lines leaves every non-hole point with its degree, removes all incidences at holes, and creates no duplicate or overlapping lines.
- **claim-ca-representation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-ca-representation)): For every field $K$ and group $G$, the assignment $c\mapsto T_c$ is a faithful unital $K$-algebra homomorphism from $K[G]$ to the algebra of $K$-linear endomorphisms of $K^G$, with composition as multiplication. In particular, $$\label{eq:ca-composition} T_c\circ T_d=T_{cd},\qquad T_1=\mathrm{id}_{K^G}.$$
  - Explanation: Group-algebra elements act faithfully and multiplicatively as linear operators on all configurations, with the product order matching the right-neighbor convention.
- **claim-ca-transfer** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-ca-transfer)): Let $K$ be a finite field, $G$ a group, and $a,b\in K[G]$ satisfy $ab=1$. Put $M=\operatorname{supp}(b)$ and define $$\label{eq:ca-rule} \phi:K^M\longrightarrow K, \qquad \phi(p)=\sum_{u\in M}b_up(u).$$ The cellular automaton $\tau=T_b$ is injective, and $$\label{eq:ca-image} \operatorname{im}(\tau)=\{y\in K^G:T_{ba}y=y\}.$$ If $ba\ne1$, every $h\in G$ with $(ba-1)_h\ne0$ gives a configuration $\delta_h\notin\operatorname{im}(\tau)$; hence $\tau$ is not surjective.
  - Explanation: A one-sided inverse over a finite field gives an injective cellular automaton whose image is the fixed set of the reverse product; a nonzero reverse defect gives a point-indicator configuration outside the image.
- **claim-nonsurjunctive-group** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-nonsurjunctive)): For the field $K$, group $G$, and outputs of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the cellular automaton $T_{b_{\mathrm{out}}}:K^G\to K^G$ is injective and not surjective. Its image is the fixed set of $T_{b_{\mathrm{out}}a_{\mathrm{out}}}$. In particular, $G$ is not surjunctive.
  - Explanation: For the constructed field, group and outputs, the associated cellular automaton is injective but not surjective, so the group is not surjunctive and Gottschalk's conjecture fails.
- **claim-f-infinity-transport** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-finfty-counterexample)): For the finite characteristic-two field $K$ of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, there are a group $U$ of type $F_\infty$ and scalar elements $A,B\in K[U]$ such that $AB=1$ and $BA\ne1$. The group $U$ is nonsofic and nonsurjunctive.
  - Explanation: Embedding the finitely presented group into a group of finiteness type F-infinity transports the scalar counterexample, and nonsoficity and nonsurjunctivity pass to the overgroup.

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-hypergraph-direction-spaces",
        "exact_quote": "There are two-dimensional subspaces $W_r\\subset\\mathcal A$, one per bundle, and $(b-1)$-dimensional subspaces $E_j\\subset\\mathcal A$, one per index, such that any at most ten distinct members of this list have direct sum. Choose a direction $e_r$ in each $W_r$, and assign its other $q$ directions bijectively to the indices in that bundle, calling them $d_j$. Then $U_j=d_j\\oplus E_j$ has dimension $b$, and $$\\label{eq:hypergraph-intersections}\n U_j\\cap U_{j'}=0\\quad(j\\ne j'),\\qquad\n U_j\\cap e_r=0,\\qquad\n U_j\\cap W_r=\n \\begin{cases}d_j,&r=\\rho(j),\\\\0,&r\\ne\\rho(j).\n \\end{cases}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-197.2"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-ca-transfer",
        "exact_quote": "Let $K$ be a finite field, $G$ a group, and $a,b\\in K[G]$ satisfy $ab=1$. Put $M=\\operatorname{supp}(b)$ and define $$\\label{eq:ca-rule}\n  \\phi:K^M\\longrightarrow K,\n  \\qquad \\phi(p)=\\sum_{u\\in M}b_up(u).$$ The cellular automaton $\\tau=T_b$ is injective, and $$\\label{eq:ca-image}\n  \\operatorname{im}(\\tau)=\\{y\\in K^G:T_{ba}y=y\\}.$$ If $ba\\ne1$, every $h\\in G$ with $(ba-1)_h\\ne0$ gives a configuration $\\delta_h\\notin\\operatorname{im}(\\tau)$; hence $\\tau$ is not surjective.",
        "source_id": "PM-197.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-ca-transfer",
          "exact_quote": "Let $K$ be a finite field, $G$ a group, and $a,b\\in K[G]$ satisfy $ab=1$.           "source_id": "PM-197.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $K$ be a finite field, $G$ a group, and $a,b\\in K[G]$ satisfy $ab=1$. Put $M=\\operatorname{supp}(b)$ and define $$\\label{eq:ca-rule}\n  \\phi:K^M\\longrightarrow K,\n  \\qquad \\phi(p)=\\sum_{u\\in M}b_up(u).$$ The cellular automaton $\\tau=T_b$ is injective, and $$\\label{eq:ca-image}\n  \\operatorname{im}(\\tau)=\\{y\\in K^G:T_{ba}y=y\\}.$$ If $ba\\ne1$, every $h\\in G$ with $(ba-1)_h\\ne0$ gives a configuration $\\delta_h\\notin\\operatorname{im}(\\tau)$; hence $\\tau$ is not surjective.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **directly finite and stably finite ring** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A unital ring with this property is *directly finite*; it is *stably finite* if all its finite square matrix rings are directly finite.
- **sofic group** ([source](#pm-h-4)): In the finite-map formulation of Elek and Szabó (Elek and Szabó 2004, Definition 4.2) [[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)] [[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)], a group $\Gamma$ is *sofic* if, for every finite $E\subseteq\Gamma$ and every $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, there are a finite nonempty $\Omega$ and a map $\sigma:\Gamma\to\Omega^\Omega$ such that $$\begin{aligned} d_\Omega\bigl(\sigma(gh),\sigma(g)\sigma(h)\bigr)&\le\varepsilon && (g,h\in E),\\ d_\Omega\bigl(\sigma(1),\mathrm{id}\bigr)&\le\varepsilon,\\ d_\Omega\bigl(\sigma(g),\mathrm{id}\bigr)&>1-\varepsilon && (g\in E\setminus\{1\}), \end{aligned}$$ where products of maps denote composition.
- **surjunctive group** ([source](#pm-h-5)): The group $G$ is *surjunctive* if every injective cellular automaton on $A^G$ is surjective, for every such $A$.
- **girth** ([source](#pm-h-7)): The girth is the length of a shortest cycle, and is infinite for a forest.
- **affine Q-line** ([source](#pm-h-7)): An affine $Q$-line is a translate of a one-dimensional $Q$-subspace.
- **direction** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A direction is such a linear subspace, not a chosen nonzero vector.
- **glued group N** ([source](#pm-h-10)): Let $N$ be the group with these generators and relators.
- **field and prime parameters of the hypergraph** ([source](#eq:hypergraph-growth)): Let $q=2^h$ and let $\ell$ be the least prime congruent to $-1$ modulo $q^{100}$.
- **bundle** ([source](#pm-h-19)): Divide an index set of size $D$ into $D/q$ bundles of size $q$.
- **exceptional rectangle** ([source](#pm-h-20)): An *exceptional rectangle* has corners $x,x+u,x+u+v,x+v$, where $0\ne u\in e_r$, $0\ne v\in e_{r'}$, and $r\ne r'$.
- **replacement lines** ([source](#pm-h-20)): The *replacement lines of label $R_r$* are all the lines of direction $e_r$ in each $o+W_r$, except $o+e_r$ itself.
- **slab** ([source](#pm-h-21)): The translates of $d_j\oplus P_{j,C}$ partition $C$ into three-dimensional affine *slabs*.
- **generic and replacement steps** ([source](#pm-h-21)): We call $G_j$ steps *generic* and $R_r$ steps *replacement* steps; a replacement step vector will also be called exceptional.
- **group of type F-infinity** ([source](#pm-h-31)): A group has type $F_\infty$ if it admits a classifying CW complex with finitely many cells in every dimension.

| q-slab-inclusion-probability | probability p_* that a fixed vector lies in the random plane | ≤ | $2q^{3-b}$ | random slab choice |
| q-hole-occupancy | holes in a coset translate | ≤ | $100$ | hole lemma property |
| q-event-sum | sum of bad-event probabilities at a variable | = | $O(q^{-7})$ | large h |
| q-local-lemma-threshold | sum of event probabilities at a variable | ≤ | $1/16$ | local lemma |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-lem-interpolation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-interpolation): Newton-type products give a triangular evaluation matrix with nonzero diagonal. -> evaluation is an isomorphism on the index simplex
- **step-line-sums** [claim](#stmt-lem-line-sums) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-line-sums): Character-sum cancellation on each monomial, then partition space into parallel lines. -> vanishing sums on lines and on the whole space
- **step-pairing** [claim](#stmt-prop-pairing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-pairing): Interpolate indicators of holes, form rank-one products, subtract the hole contribution, check sizes. -> vectors with the total and line pairing identities
- **step-embedding** [claim](#stmt-lem-embedding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-embedding): Encode a word equality as a planar bipartite graph and contradict Euler's formula using girth. -> injectivity of local group maps
- **step-glued-group** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#pm-h-10): Glue copies of the local groups along line subgroups into a finitely presented group. -> group N with embedded local subgroups
- **step-rectangular-identity** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#pm-h-12): Subgroup averages and the pairing vectors give rectangular matrices with forward product the identity. -> UP=U, PT=T, UT=I
- **step-hnn-extension** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#pm-h-13): Multiple extension with character twists; embedding of the base group by a permutation representation. -> group L with orthogonal character idempotents
- **step-packing** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#pm-h-14): Pack coordinates into slots; orthogonality removes cross terms; complement of the central idempotent completes the square factors. -> square matrices with forward product the identity
- **step-reverse-defect** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#pm-h-15): Sandwich the reverse defect and specialize on the subgroup algebra to a matrix with a nonzero kernel witness. -> nonvanishing reverse defect
- **step-scalar-elements** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#pm-h-16): Embed the matrix algebra via matrix units of an auxiliary finite group and add the complementary block identity. -> scalar pair with the product relations
- **step-finite-presentation** [claim](#stmt-thm-criterion) / [proof](#sec:finite-presentation): Track presentations through the product and extension constructions and note the retained odd torsion. -> finite presentation and odd-order element
- **step-direction-spaces** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-direction-spaces) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-direction-spaces): Vandermonde independence over an extension field. -> independent bundle and index spaces
- **step-holes** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-holes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-holes): Random selection, Chebyshev, union and Markov bounds, then deletion of marked points. -> compatible hole set
- **step-replacement-lines** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-replacement-lines) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-replacement-lines): Use the plane-occupancy property of holes and the rectangle-witness property. -> disjoint replacement lines without exceptional rectangles
- **step-polygons** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-polygons) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-polygons): Closure and independence of direction spaces force each generic label to occur twice. -> three rigid polygon forms with independent variables
- **step-event-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-event-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-event-bounds): Count events per variable for hole pairs, generic rectangles, mixed rectangles and pentagons. -> small sum of probabilities per variable
- **step-local-lemma** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-local-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-local-lemma): Conditional probability induction with weights twice the event probabilities. -> positive probability of avoiding all events
- **step-slab-partition** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-slab-partition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-slab-partition): Parallel lines, or lines given by an operator without eigenvectors around a designated axis. -> line partition of a slab
- **step-local-trade** [claim](#stmt-lem-hypergraph-trade) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-trade): Replace each hole fan by parallel lines and count incidence changes. -> regular line family avoiding holes
- **step-hypergraph-girth** [claim](#stmt-thm-hypergraph) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-trade-2): Show any short incidence cycle is an excluded polygon or a replacement rectangle. -> hole set and line family of the required girth
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hypergraph-trade-3): Finite search for the incidence data, then apply the pairing proposition and the criterion. -> field, group and the two finite sums
- **step-ca-representation** [claim](#stmt-lem-ca-representation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ca-representation): Regroup finite sums to show multiplicativity; read coefficients from point indicators. -> faithful unital algebra homomorphism
- **step-ca-transfer** [claim](#stmt-prop-ca-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-ca-transfer): Left inverse gives injectivity; image equals fixed set of the reverse product; indicator witness. -> injective, nonsurjective automaton
- **step-nonsurjunctive** [claim](#stmt-cor-nonsurjunctive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-nonsurjunctive): Apply the transfer proposition to the constructed outputs. -> nonsurjunctive group
- **step-f-infinity** [claim](#stmt-cor-finfty-counterexample) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-finfty-counterexample): Embed the group in a group of type F-infinity and push the elements forward by a unital injection. -> counterexample in a group of type F-infinity

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-one-sided-inverse-pair**: The constructed field, finitely presented group and two finite group-algebra sums satisfy a_out b_out = 1 while b_out a_out is not 1, so the group algebra is not directly finite.
- **inv-nonsofic-group**: The group carrying the pair is nonsofic, because a sofic group would force direct finiteness of its group algebra over this field.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-finite-dimensional-algebras**: Citing the counterexample to doubt left-inverse implies right-inverse for ordinary finite-dimensional matrix or linear-map computations. -> In finite dimension a left inverse is automatically a right inverse, so the failure only occurs in an infinite-dimensional group algebra.
- **anti-sofic-groups**: Applying the construction to claim failure of direct finiteness for a sofic group. -> Sofic groups give stably finite group algebras, so no such pair exists there.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is a terminating prescription whose field, group and sums are existentially guaranteed by a search over huge finite sets, and the source states that it need not be computationally practical and has not been executed. No small finite instance exists to run, so no honest executable reference with an independent oracle can be given.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existence theorem with a terminating but impractical prescription that the paper says has not been executed. No runnable library follows from it. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The cellular automaton lives on an infinite group's configuration space and has no finite grid. Nothing can be simulated step by step, so it gives no game or simulation mechanic. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper offers an existence proof, not a tool. Its sums are defined without any procedure that normalizes group words, so a computer-algebra tool would have no usable normal form. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A finite lexicographic search is described, but it ranges over huge sets and has no complexity analysis. Its termination rests on Linnik's theorem and the large-parameter theorem. It is a definability device, not an implementable algorithm. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The incidence systems are finite, but they serve only as proof scaffolding. No data structure with operations or costs is proposed. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finite-field arithmetic appears only to define the field in the prescription. No parallel or hardware-oriented computation is given. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction relies on characteristic two and odd-order torsion to make certain scalars vanish or be invertible. It states no hardness assumption and no security property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The linear cellular automaton is a purely algebraic object on an infinite configuration space. No learning problem, data set or training objective is involved. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The probabilistic step only proves that a configuration with no bad event exists. The paper states that no resampling algorithm is needed, so no optimisation procedure arises. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns communication, consensus or replication. Cellular automata here live on a group's configuration space, not on networked nodes. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural homes are pure algebra and symbolic dynamics, where it is a counterexample to Kaplansky's and Gottschalk's conjectures. The positive-result literature in characteristic zero blocks any transfer of the counterexample there. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-char-two-finite-field** (hypothesis): The counterexample requires a finite field of characteristic two. The paper does not assert that the field is the prime field of order two, and it says nothing about other characteristics or about infinite fields. Blocked use: ['Citing the main theorem for a group algebra over a field of characteristic zero or of odd characteristic', 'Assuming the field is the prime field of order two']
- **limit-odd-torsion-required** (hypothesis): The group must contain an element of odd prime order. The averages over local subgroups need invertible denominators, so the argument depends on odd-order torsion and gives no statement about torsion-free groups. Blocked use: ['Using this paper alone to claim failure of direct finiteness for a torsion-free group']
- **limit-girth-twelve-embedding** (hypothesis): The embedding of the local additive groups into the glued group rests on incidence girth at least twelve. Without that girth the planar-graph contradiction with Euler's formula is not available, so the local groups may collapse. Blocked use: ['Applying the incidence criterion to a line system with shorter cycles', 'Assuming local subgroups embed for an arbitrary incidence structure']
- **limit-size-inequality-defect** (hypothesis): The size condition m<t<=m kappa is used twice. The upper bound allows packing into square matrices, and the strict inequality m<t makes the reverse defect nonzero. If either fails, neither the packing nor the defect certificate is available. Blocked use: ['Running the criterion on vector data with t at most m', 'Packing more coordinates than the available slot and character pairs']
- **limit-pairing-field-and-hole-regime** (hypothesis): The pairing identities are proved for a field of order a power of two with order at least four, holes inside the index simplex, and hole counts within the stated density window. The degree bound q-2 for line sums is essential. Blocked use: ['Using the pairing proposition over fields of odd order', 'Using hole sets outside the stated density window']
- **limit-existence-for-large-h-only** (nonconstructive): The line-system theorem holds only for all sufficiently large field parameter, with an unspecified threshold. The existence of holes and slab choices is shown by probabilistic arguments, and the local lemma gives only positive probability, not a procedure. Blocked use: ['Claiming a specific small parameter admits the required line system', 'Treating the random construction as an algorithm']
- **limit-prescription-not-practical** (formal_scope): The finite prescription terminates, but it is not claimed to be computationally practical and has not been executed. No explicit size of the field, group or sums is given, and none can be assumed. Blocked use: ['Quoting a running time or an explicit instance of the counterexample', 'Asserting the search was run to completion']
- **limit-linnik-prime-constants** (approximation): The prime ell is controlled only through an unspecified absolute-constant bound from a theorem on least primes in progressions. The relation of s to h therefore depends on constants not made explicit. Blocked use: ['Computing explicit sizes of the line system from the Linnik bound', 'Treating the constants in the least-prime bound as known']
- **limit-reverse-defect-subgroup-only** (edge_case): The specialisation homomorphism is applied only on the subgroup algebra and is not asserted to extend to the whole group algebra. The nonzero reverse defect is certified there, not by any evaluation in the full algebra. Blocked use: ['Extending the scalar specialisation to the full group algebra', 'Deciding group-word equalities by this evaluation']
- **limit-ca-transfer-finite-field** (hypothesis): The automaton transfer needs a finite alphabet, so a finite coefficient field, and a one-sided inverse pair. The product convention matches right neighbours, and the image statement holds for the stated convention only. Blocked use: ['Applying the transfer over an infinite field to claim a finite-alphabet automaton', 'Swapping the product order without changing the convention']
- **limit-finfty-external-theorem** (formal_scope): The transport to a group of type F-infinity depends on an external embedding theorem for finitely presented groups that is cited, not proved here, and it needs the finite presentation of the constructed group. Blocked use: ['Claiming the type F-infinity result without the cited embedding theorem']

Scope note of family 197 (`lean/docs/197.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A Counterexample to Kaplansky's Direct-Finiteness Conjecture in Characteristic Two](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-Kaplanskys-Direct-Finiteness-Conjecture-in-Characteristic-Two-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A Counterexample to the Group-Ring Determinant Conjecture](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-the-Group-Ring-Determinant-Conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A Counterexample to Kaplansky's Direct-Finiteness Conjecture in Odd Characteristic](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-Kaplanskys-Direct-Finiteness-Conjecture-in-Odd-Characteristic-September-26-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture asserts that $ab=1$ implies $ba=1$ for $a,b\in K[G]$, for every field $K$ and group $G$. The formalized results construct a finite field of characteristic two and a group algebra violating this implication. One statement records the counterexample for a finitely generated group; the more detailed construction gives a finitely presented group with an element of odd prime order.
>
> The detailed construction also gives a precise recipe for choosing the data and proves that the required search terminates. The further conclusion that the group is nonsofic is outside these statements.
>
> A nonsingular integer matrix has absolute determinant at least $1$. The group-ring Determinant Conjecture extends this bound to matrices over $\mathbb Z[G]$ for every discrete group $G$. If $T_A$ is the operator induced by such a matrix $A$ on finite direct sums of $\ell^2(G)$, and $\mu_A$ is the spectral measure of $T_A^*T_A$ with respect to the group trace, the conjecture asserts
>
> $\displaystyle \int_{(0,\infty)}\log t\,d\mu_A(t)\ge0,$
>
> with the zero spectral atom omitted. For an invertible square matrix, this is equivalent to its Fuglede–Kadison determinant being at least $1$.
>
> The formalized result contradicts that bound. It gives a finitely generated group $G$ and an $n\times n$ matrix over $\mathbb Z[G]$, with $n\ge1$, that is invertible over $\mathbb Q[G]$. Its bounded left-regular operator is invertible and has determinant strictly between $0$ and $1$.
>
> The formalization uses the trace of $\log(T^*T)$ to define the determinant for the invertible operator $T$. The paper's additional spectral-measure integral conclusion is not included.
>
> Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture asserts that $ab=1$ implies $ba=1$ in every group algebra over a field. Let $p$ be the smallest prime divisor of $\bigl(\binom{1200}{600}!\bigr)^2+1$; this prime is odd. The formalized result constructs a field $K$ of order $p^4$, a finitely generated group $G$ with torsion, and elements $a,b$ violating this implication. The associated cellular automaton on all configurations $K^G$ is injective but not surjective.
>
> The formalization also contains fixed-field transfer results, separate from the statement linked below.

|---|---|
| `MainClaim` | def |
| `main_theorem` | theorem |

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We disprove Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture by constructing a finite field <i>K</i> of characteristic two, a finitely presented group <i>G</i>, and finite sums $`a,b\in K[G]`$ with $`ab=1`$ but $`ba\ne1`$. The group <i>G</i> is nonsofic. The same elements define a cellular automaton on <i>K</i><sup><i>G</i></sup> that is injective but not surjective, disproving Gottschalk's surjunctivity conjecture.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

If a linear map on a finite-dimensional vector space has a left inverse, that inverse is also a right inverse. A group algebra need not have finite dimension, but each of its elements still has finite support. Kaplansky’s direct-finiteness conjecture asks whether this finite support is enough: for every field $K$, group $G$, and $a,b\in K[G]$, must $ab=1$ imply $ba=1$? Here $K[G]$ consists of finite formal sums $\sum_g c_g g$, multiplied using the group law.

A unital ring with this property is *directly finite*; it is *stably finite* if all its finite square matrix rings are directly finite. This distinction matters to a counterexample. Producing matrices $AB=I\ne BA$ over a group algebra does not by itself produce scalar elements with the same property. Our construction first produces the matrix pair, then embeds its matrix algebra in a larger group algebra to obtain the following scalar conclusion.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exist a finite field $K$ of characteristic two, a finitely presented group $G$ containing an element of odd prime order, and elements $a_{\mathrm{out}},b_{\mathrm{out}}\in K[G]$ such that $$a_{\mathrm{out}}b_{\mathrm{out}}=1,
\qquad b_{\mathrm{out}}a_{\mathrm{out}}\ne1.$$ The field, group, and finite sums are specified by a terminating prescription involving finite sets and finite-field arithmetic.*

</div>

Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the unrestricted conjecture negatively in characteristic two. The construction uses odd-order torsion and does not assert that $K=\mathbb F_2$. The prescription need not be computationally practical and has not been executed: its termination is proved, and the two product assertions have algebraic certificates independent of any decision procedure for the group word problem.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### History and positive results.

For a finite group $G$, the algebra $K[G]$ and all its finite square matrix rings are finite dimensional, so elementary linear algebra gives stable finiteness. The classical characteristic-zero theorem uses a different substitute for dimension. Kaplansky’s operator-algebra argument places $\mathbb C[G]$ in a finite tracial setting; Montgomery gave a proof using the operator-norm closure of the regular representation and its identity-coefficient trace, including the extension to every finite matrix size (Montgomery 1969) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Montgomery1969](#ref-Montgomery1969)]. The trace rules out a proper one-sided inverse. For an arbitrary characteristic-zero field, all coefficients of a proposed finite matrix pair lie in a finitely generated subfield, which embeds in $\mathbb C$. Thus the complex result gives stable finiteness over every characteristic-zero field.

In positive characteristic, rank and approximation methods have supplied broad classes of positive results without a complex representation. Ara, O’Meara, and Perera proved stable finiteness over arbitrary division rings for free-by-amenable groups, using Sylvester rank functions and amenable translation rings (Ara, O’Meara, and Perera 2002) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[AraOMearaPerera2002](#ref-AraOMearaPerera2002)]. Elek and Szabó obtained stable finiteness over arbitrary division rings for all sofic groups from finite approximate models and a rank construction (Elek and Szabó 2004, Corollary 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)]. These are restrictions on a possible counterexample’s group, not assumptions that the group constructed here satisfies.

Dykema and Juschenko clarified how a matrix obstruction can nevertheless yield a scalar one: for a fixed field and group, stable finiteness is equivalent to direct finiteness after taking the direct product with every finite group (Dykema and Juschenko 2015, Theorem 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[DykemaJuschenko2015](#ref-DykemaJuschenko2015)]. Their reduction embeds a matrix algebra in a finite-group algebra and adds the complementary identity. We realize this transfer explicitly for the field used here, giving both the matrix units and the resulting two elements.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Finite presentation and soficity.

For a finite nonempty set $\Omega$, write $d_\Omega(f,h)$ for the fraction of points on which two maps $f,h:\Omega\to\Omega$ differ. In the finite-map formulation of Elek and Szabó (Elek and Szabó 2004, Definition 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)] <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)], a group $\Gamma$ is *sofic* if, for every finite $E\subseteq\Gamma$ and every $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, there are a finite nonempty $\Omega$ and a map $\sigma:\Gamma\to\Omega^\Omega$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
d_\Omega\bigl(\sigma(gh),\sigma(g)\sigma(h)\bigr)&\le\varepsilon
&& (g,h\in E),\\
d_\Omega\bigl(\sigma(1),\mathrm{id}\bigr)&\le\varepsilon,\\
d_\Omega\bigl(\sigma(g),\mathrm{id}\bigr)&>1-\varepsilon
&& (g\in E\setminus\{1\}),
\end{aligned}$$ where products of maps denote composition. Thus soficity asks for finite approximate models of multiplication in which nonidentity elements act almost without fixed points.

The stable-finiteness theorem of Elek and Szabó (Elek and Szabó 2004, Corollary 4.7) applies to the field $K$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. If its particular group $G$ were sofic, then $K[G]$ would be stably finite and hence directly finite by the matrix-size-one case, contradicting the two displayed product assertions. Therefore this same $G$ is nonsofic. In particular, the construction supplies a finitely presented counterexample to the assertion that every group is sofic. The finite presentation is established directly in Section <a href="#sec:finite-presentation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:finite-presentation">4.6</a>; the nonsoficity conclusion uses only the scalar defect and the cited theorem.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Cellular automata.

For a nonempty finite alphabet $A$, a configuration is an arbitrary function $x:G\to A$. A *cellular automaton* on $A^G$ is a map given by a finite set $M\subset G$ and a local rule $\phi:A^M\to A$ through $$\tau(x)(g)=\phi\bigl((x(gu))_{u\in M}\bigr).$$ The group $G$ is *surjunctive* if every injective cellular automaton on $A^G$ is surjective, for every such $A$. Gottschalk’s conjecture asserts that every group is surjunctive (Gottschalk 1973) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Gottschalk1973](#ref-Gottschalk1973)]. The problem asks whether finite local dependence and translation symmetry preserve the finite-set implication from injectivity to surjectivity on the infinite configuration space.

Gromov developed a general symbolic-algebraic surjunctivity theorem (Gromov 1999, sec. 7.G$'$) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Gromov1999](#ref-Gromov1999)]; Weiss gave the finite-alphabet sofic-group theorem directly (Weiss 2000, Theorem 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Weiss2000](#ref-Weiss2000)]. Elek and Szabó explicitly recorded the implication from surjunctivity to direct finiteness over finite fields (Elek and Szabó 2004, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)]. Ceccherini-Silberstein and Coornaert developed the linear theory: their equivalence, quantified over all coefficient fields, relates stable finiteness to injective-to-surjective behavior of linear cellular automata with finite-dimensional vector alphabets (Ceccherini-Silberstein and Coornaert 2006, Theorem 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CSCCoornaert2006](#ref-CSCCoornaert2006)]. Phung proved stable finiteness over every field for surjunctive groups, using algebraic cellular automata (Phung 2023, Theorem B) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Phung2023](#ref-Phung2023)]. Ceccherini-Silberstein, Coornaert, and Phung later gave a model-theoretic proof and a monoid extension (Ceccherini-Silberstein, Coornaert, and Phung 2025a; Ceccherini-Silberstein, Coornaert, and Phung 2025b, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[CSCPhungGroup2025](#ref-CSCPhungGroup2025), [CSCPhungMonoid2025](#ref-CSCPhungMonoid2025)]. These results connect algebraic finiteness to a dynamical property rather than imposing finite dimensionality.

For the finite field in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the short algebra-to-automaton passage can be given directly. The coefficients of $b_{\mathrm{out}}$ define a local rule, the coefficients of $a_{\mathrm{out}}$ give a left inverse on all configurations, and a nonzero coefficient of $b_{\mathrm{out}}a_{\mathrm{out}}-1$ identifies a missing point-indicator configuration. Section <a href="#sec:cellular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cellular">7</a> proves this standard transfer in full, including the multiplication convention. It gives a negative resolution of Gottschalk’s conjecture for the group in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>; it requires no further existence theorem or approximation assumption.

The characteristic-two scalar pair in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is also the input to the companion counterexample to the group-ring Determinant Conjecture (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DeterminantCompanion](#ref-DeterminantCompanion)]. By a separate construction, that paper produces an integral square matrix over another finitely generated group, invertible over its rational group ring, whose Fuglede–Kadison determinant lies strictly between zero and one.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### The construction.

The obstacle is to force a one-sided inverse while retaining a certificate that the reverse product is not the identity. The proof separates these requirements through finite incidence data. Let $\mathcal F$ be a family of subsets of a finite set $V$. We require that each point lie in $\ell+1$ members, where $\ell$ is an odd prime, and that the bipartite incidence graph have girth at least $12$. We also require column vectors $x_v\in K^m$ satisfying $$\sum_{v\in f}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=0\quad(f\in\mathcal F),
\qquad \sum_{v\in V}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=-I_m,
\qquad m<|V|\le m\ell^2.$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:criterion">6</a> turns these finite data into a counterexample.

Two parts of this criterion explain the separation. First, a planar balance argument proves that copies of the additive group $\mathbb F_\ell^2$, glued along their one-dimensional subgroups according to the incidence structure, embed in the resulting group. Incidence girth forces a contradiction with Euler’s formula for any diagram witnessing a collapsed local element. Second, character idempotents and prescribed subgroup conjugations pack a rectangular factorization into square matrices. Their forward product is the identity by the displayed pairings.

To detect the reverse-product defect, we sandwich it by the packing maps and obtain a matrix in an embedded subgroup algebra. A homomorphism from that algebra to $K$ sends this matrix to $X^{\mathsf T}X-I_{|V|}$, where $X$ has columns $x_v$. Since $X$ has only $m<|V|$ rows, $X^{\mathsf T}X$ cannot be the identity, so the original defect is nonzero. The homomorphism is used only on the subgroup algebra; it is not asserted to extend to the larger group algebra. A finite auxiliary group then converts the matrix obstruction into the scalar outputs of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

To produce the incidence data, we take full affine lines over a finite field $Q\subset K$ and remove a small set of points. Low-degree interpolation on these holes gives the vectors $x_v$: the pairings sum to zero on each full line, but removing the holes leaves the nonzero total $-I_m$. The geometric task is to retain a regular line system of large incidence girth. We combine random small-dimensional slabs with an exact trade in each bundle of directions through a hole. Hole selection and a weighted local-lemma estimate rule out triangles, rectangles, and pentagons of lines; the local trades preserve regularity. A uniform bound from Linnik’s theorem supplies compatible parameters (Xylouris 2011, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Xylouris2011](#ref-Xylouris2011)].

Low-degree polynomial products and affine-line restrictions also occur in the coding-theoretic constructions of Golowich–Lin and Dinur–Liu–Zhang (Golowich and Lin 2024; Dinur, Liu, and Zhang 2025) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GolowichLin2024](#ref-GolowichLin2024), [DinurLiuZhang2025](#ref-DinurLiuZhang2025)]: degree bounds add under products, while restriction to a line does not increase degree. Here these features produce the line and total pairing identities. We use no coding-theoretic theorem from those works. The only non-elementary existence theorem imported into the direct construction is the uniform least-prime bound; the group embeddings, finite matrix block, interpolation, and probability argument needed here are proved below.

Section <a href="#sec:pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pairing">2</a> first explains the polynomial pairing and the two size bounds. Section <a href="#sec:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:criterion">3</a> formulates the algebraic criterion and proves that its local groups embed. Section <a href="#sec:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packing">4</a> completes the passage from subgroup averages to scalar elements, including a certificate for the reverse defect. Section <a href="#sec:hypergraph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hypergraph">5</a> then supplies the required affine-line system by the full probability and local-trade argument. Section <a href="#sec:prescription" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prescription">6</a> fixes all choices and completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Section <a href="#sec:cellular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cellular">7</a> gives the cellular automaton and determines its image by finite local equations.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Conventions.

All incidence structures in the proof are finite. Their incidence graphs have one vertex for each point and one for each member of the family, with an edge for each membership. The girth is the length of a shortest cycle, and is infinite for a forest. An affine $Q$-line is a translate of a one-dimensional $Q$-subspace. A direction is such a linear subspace, not a chosen nonzero vector. Superscript $\mathsf T$ denotes transpose, without any involution on the coefficient field. All group presentations define abstract discrete groups.

<a id="sec:pairing"></a>
### Polynomial pairings on a punctured affine space

The pairing comes from low-degree polynomials that distinguish the points to be deleted. Their pairwise products sum to zero on every full affine line and on the whole affine space. At the deleted points, however, the evaluation vectors are coordinate vectors, so their contribution is the identity matrix. Removing these points leaves its negative as the total pairing on the remaining space.

The interpolation argument does not depend on how the retained full lines are arranged. Regularity and girth enter as separate geometric hypotheses in the proposition below. Section <a href="#sec:hypergraph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hypergraph">5</a> will supply them, after Sections <a href="#sec:criterion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:criterion">3</a> and <a href="#sec:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packing">4</a> explain their algebraic use.

We use the usual lower-set Newton interpolation argument; compare Dyn and Floater (Dyn and Floater 2014) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[DynFloater2014](#ref-DynFloater2014)]. The triangular evaluation proof below works directly over the finite field needed here. The later products of interpolants and their restrictions to full affine lines are the features related to the coding constructions of Golowich–Lin (Golowich and Lin 2024, secs. 2.1.2–2.1.3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[GolowichLin2024](#ref-GolowichLin2024)] and Dinur–Liu–Zhang (Dinur, Liu, and Zhang 2025, secs. 3.4–3.5) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[DinurLiuZhang2025](#ref-DinurLiuZhang2025)]. We need no coding theorem from either work: the degree bound and all line identities used in this paper are proved below.

<a id="stmt-lem-interpolation"></a>
<div id="lem:interpolation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Interpolation on an index simplex). *Let $Q$ be a field of order $q$, enumerate its elements as $a_0,\ldots,a_{q-1}$, and let $0\le d<q$. For $n\ge1$, set $$S_d=\{(a_{k_1},\ldots,a_{k_n}): k_i\ge0,\ \textstyle\sum_i k_i\le d\}\subset Q^n.$$ Evaluation on $S_d$ is an isomorphism from the $Q$-vector space of polynomials of total degree at most $d$ to $Q^{S_d}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-interpolation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-interpolation</summary>

*Proof.* For each tuple $k=(k_1,\ldots,k_n)$ of total sum at most $d$, consider $$N_k(X_1,\ldots,X_n)=\prod_{i=1}^n\prod_{0\le u<k_i}(X_i-a_u).$$ Its evaluation at the point with index tuple $j$ vanishes if $j_i<k_i$ for any $i$. Thus, with tuples in lexicographic order, the square evaluation matrix is triangular. Its diagonal entry indexed by $k$ is $$\prod_{i=1}^n\prod_{0\le u<k_i}(a_{k_i}-a_u)\ne0.$$ Since $d<q$, there is no additional coordinate restriction on these tuples. Their number is $\binom{d+n}{n}$, the dimension of the polynomial space. The displayed polynomials therefore give an invertible evaluation map. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-line-sums"></a>
<div id="lem:line-sums" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Line sums). *Let $n\ge1$. If $P$ is a polynomial on $Q^n$ of total degree at most $q-2$, then its sum on every full affine $Q$-line is zero. Its sum on $Q^n$ is also zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-line-sums"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-line-sums</summary>

*Proof.* On a line, write its restriction as a polynomial in a parameter $z\in Q$ of degree at most $q-2$. The sum of the constant monomial is $q=0$ in $Q$. If $1\le k<q-1$, choose $c\in Q^\times$ with $c^k\ne1$, using cyclicity of $Q^\times$. Multiplication by $c$ permutes $Q$, so $$\sum_{z\in Q}z^k=c^k\sum_{z\in Q}z^k$$ forces the sum to vanish. More explicitly, $(1-c^k)\sum_z z^k=0$ and $1-c^k\ne0$. This proves the line assertion. Partition $Q^n$ into parallel lines to obtain the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-pairing"></a>
<div id="prop:pairing" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:hole-size"></a>
**Proposition 4** (The pairing supplied by holes). *Let $q\ge4$ be a power of two, let $Q$ be a field of order $q$, fix an enumeration of $Q$, and let $n\ge1$. Put $d_0=(q-2)/2$, $S=S_{d_0}\subset\mathcal A=Q^n$, and $N_{\rm pt}=q^n$. Let $\ell$ be an odd prime and $D=\ell+1$. Suppose that $O\subset S$ and that $\mathcal F$ is a family of full affine $Q$-lines contained in $V=\mathcal A\setminus O$, with $$\label{eq:hole-size}
\frac{2N_{\rm pt}}{D^2}\le |O|\le\frac{7N_{\rm pt}}{D^2}.$$ Suppose also that every point of $V$ lies on $D$ members of $\mathcal F$ and that the incidence graph has girth at least $12$. If $K$ is a finite extension of $Q$ containing a primitive $\ell$th root of unity, then, with $m=|O|$ and $t=|V|$, there are columns $x_v\in K^m$, $v\in V$, such that $$0<m<t\le m\ell^2,\qquad
\sum_{v\in V}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=-I_m,\qquad
\sum_{v\in f}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=0\quad(f\in\mathcal F).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pairing"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pairing</summary>

<a id="eq:polynomial-pairing"></a>
*Proof.* Interpolation is on the whole set $S$, although only the subset $O$ is deleted. For each $o\in O$, Lemma <a href="#lem:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:interpolation">2</a> gives a unique polynomial $f_o$ of total degree at most $d_0$ whose values on $S$ are the indicator function of $o$. Order $O$ and define, for all $v\in\mathcal A$, $$x_v=(f_o(v))_{o\in O}\in Q^m\subset K^m,
\qquad W_v=x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}.$$ Each entry of $W_v$ is the value at $v$ of a polynomial of total degree at most $2d_0=q-2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:line-sums" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:line-sums">3</a> therefore gives $$\sum_{v\in f}W_v=0\quad(f\in\mathcal F),
\qquad \sum_{v\in\mathcal A}W_v=0.$$ For $o\in O$, $x_o$ is its coordinate unit vector. Hence $\sum_{o\in O}W_o=I_m$, and subtraction yields $$\label{eq:polynomial-pairing}
\sum_{v\in V}W_v=-I_m,
\qquad \sum_{v\in f}W_v=0\quad(f\in\mathcal F).$$

It remains to check the sizes. Since $D\ge4$, the upper bound in <a href="#eq:hole-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hole-size">[eq:hole-size]</a> is strictly less than $N_{\rm pt}/2$. The lower bound is positive. Thus $0<m<t$, and $$\frac{t}{m}=\frac{N_{\rm pt}}{m}-1
\le\frac{D^2}{2}-1\le(D-1)^2=\ell^2,$$ where the last inequality is equivalent to $(D-2)^2/2\ge0$. These are all the claimed identities and inequalities. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 5*. The simultaneous identities in <a href="#eq:polynomial-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-pairing">[eq:polynomial-pairing]</a> are compatible with regularity because $D=\ell+1$ is zero as a scalar in characteristic two. Summing the line identities multiplies the total point sum by $D$, rather than recovering that total point sum.

</div>

<a id="sec:criterion"></a>
### An incidence criterion and its embedded local groups

We now leave affine geometry. The following criterion is the algebraic part of the construction. Its input consists of a finite incidence system and vectors satisfying two matrix identities. All groups and group-algebra elements in its conclusion are constructed in this section and Section <a href="#sec:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:packing">4</a>; no further geometric assumption enters those arguments.

<a id="stmt-thm-criterion"></a>
<div id="thm:criterion" class="theorem">

**Theorem 6** (Incidence criterion). *Let $\ell$ be an odd prime, let $K$ be a finite field of characteristic two containing a primitive $\ell$th root of unity $\zeta$, and put $\kappa=\ell^2$. Let $V$ be a finite set of cardinality $t$, and let $\mathcal F$ be a finite family of nonempty subsets of $V$. Assume that every $v\in V$ belongs to exactly $\ell+1$ members of $\mathcal F$ and that the bipartite incidence graph of $V$ and $\mathcal F$ has girth at least $12$.*

<a id="eq:criterion-size"></a>
<a id="eq:criterion-pairing"></a>
*Suppose that an integer $m$ and column vectors $x_v\in K^m$, $v\in V$, satisfy $$\label{eq:criterion-size}
0<m<t\le m\kappa$$ and $$\label{eq:criterion-pairing}
\sum_{v\in V}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=-I_m,
\qquad
\sum_{v\in f}x_vx_v^{\mathsf T}=0
\quad\text{for every }f\in\mathcal F.$$ Then there are a finitely presented group $G$ containing an element of order $\ell$ and finite sums $a_{\mathrm{out}},b_{\mathrm{out}}\in K[G]$ such that $$a_{\mathrm{out}}b_{\mathrm{out}}=1,
\qquad
b_{\mathrm{out}}a_{\mathrm{out}}\ne1.$$ They are given by the constructions and formulas below.*

</div>

The hypotheses have separate roles. Incidence girth preserves the local subgroups, while Equation <a href="#eq:criterion-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:criterion-pairing">[eq:criterion-pairing]</a> produces a rectangular matrix identity. The upper bound $t\le m\kappa$ permits character packing into square matrices; the strict inequality $m<t$ then detects their reverse-product defect in an embedded subgroup algebra. An explicit finite group supplies matrix units that turn these matrices into the promised elements.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The glued group

We first realize the incidence data by finite subgroups of one group. For each $v\in V$, let $K_v^0$ be a coordinatized copy of the additive group of $\mathbb F_\ell^2$, and for each $f\in\mathcal F$, let $L_f^0$ be a copy of the additive group of $\mathbb F_\ell$. Since exactly $\ell+1$ hyperedges contain $v$, choose injections $$\iota_{vf}:L_f^0\longrightarrow K_v^0\qquad(v\in f)$$ whose images are the $\ell+1$ distinct one-dimensional subspaces of $K_v^0$. These are homomorphisms of additive groups. In particular, their images partition $K_v^0\setminus\{0\}$ outside their common zero element.

Introduce a generator $s_{f,c}$ for each $f\in\mathcal F$ and each $0\ne c\in L_f^0$. A signed word $$s_{f_1,c_1}^{\varepsilon_1}\cdots
  s_{f_k,c_k}^{\varepsilon_k},\qquad \varepsilon_i\in\{1,-1\},$$ is a defining relator whenever some $v\in V$ belongs to every $f_i$ and $$\sum_{i=1}^k\varepsilon_i\iota_{vf_i}(c_i)=0
  \quad\text{in }K_v^0.$$ Let $N$ be the group with these generators and relators. It is finitely generated, since the incidence data and all the local groups are finite.

There is a homomorphism $\theta_v:K_v^0\to N$ determined by $$\theta_v(0)=1,\qquad
  \theta_v\bigl(\iota_{vf}(c)\bigr)=s_{f,c}\quad(c\ne0).$$ Indeed, each nonzero element has a unique expression on the left. For $a,b\in K_v^0$, the word expressing $\theta_v(a)\theta_v(b)\theta_v(a+b)^{-1}$ is a defining relator, after omitting factors corresponding to zero elements.

The same group is presented by the finite sublist of defining relators of length at most three. These include every local multiplication-table relation just displayed, as well as the local inverse relations. Within one $K_v^0$, successive applications of those relations reduce any signed word to the generator for its sum, or to the empty word when the sum is zero. Thus they imply every defining relator of $N$, and $N$ is finitely presented. We retain the all-word formulation because it makes the planar proof below more transparent.

<a id="stmt-lem-embedding"></a>
<div id="lem:embedding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *If the incidence graph of $(V,\mathcal F)$ has girth at least $12$, then every homomorphism $\theta_v:K_v^0\to N$ is injective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-embedding"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-embedding</summary>

*Proof.* Every nonzero element of $K_v^0$ maps to a single generator $s_{f,c}$. It therefore suffices to prove that no such generator is the identity in $N$. Suppose, to the contrary, that $s_{f,c}=1$, with $c\ne0$. We will encode this equality by a planar graph with zero local sums at every vertex except one. Reducing the graph will make the local group relations force lower bounds on vertex degrees, while incidence girth forces lower bounds on facial lengths. These bounds will contradict Euler’s formula even with the one exceptional vertex.

*From a word equality to a planar graph.* The one-letter word $s_{f,c}$ can be changed to the empty word by finitely many insertions and deletions of contiguous defining relators. To justify this form of reduction, take the congruence on signed words generated by those operations. Every formal inverse pair is a defining relator, since its hyperedge has an incident vertex. The quotient is consequently a group, and its universal property is exactly that of the presentation of $N$. Thus equality in $N$ is equality under this congruence. Equivalently, each factor $u r^{\pm1}u^{-1}$ in a finite normal-closure expression can be inserted by first forming $u u^{-1}$ through nested inverse-pair insertions and then inserting $r^{\pm1}$ between its two halves. Inverses of defining relators are again defining relators.

Draw the successive words on horizontal levels, treating each signed letter as an individual token. Between two levels, connect the tokens that persist in their unchanged order. An inserted or deleted block begins or ends its tokens at a new black vertex, labelled by a point $v$ at which the corresponding relator has zero sum. This can be drawn without crossings: the affected block is contiguous, so its star occupies a single gap between the other order-preserving wires. Joining the finitely many strips gives a planar drawing.

<a id="eq:embedding-balances"></a>
Every inserted token $s_{i,y}^{\varepsilon}$ has one creation vertex and one deletion vertex. Subdivide its full lifetime wire by a white vertex labelled $i$, and give its two edges weights $\varepsilon y$ at the creation end and $-\varepsilon y$ at the deletion end. The initial token instead has an exceptional white endpoint labelled $f$, joined to its deletion vertex by an edge of weight $-c$. We obtain a finite planar bipartite multigraph with black labels in $V$ and white labels in $\mathcal F$. An edge joining labels $v$ and $i$ has a nonzero weight in $L_i^0$, and $v\in i$. Its balances are $$\label{eq:embedding-balances}
\begin{aligned}
  &\sum_{a\ni x}\iota_{v,i(a)}\bigl(\omega(a)\bigr)=0
    &&\text{at each black vertex $x$ labelled $v$},\\
  &\sum_{a\ni y}\omega(a)=0
    &&\text{at each ordinary white vertex $y$},
\end{aligned}$$ where $\omega(a)$ is the weight of edge $a$ and $i(a)$ is its white label. The exceptional white vertex has nonzero sum. At creation vertices the first balance is the relator sum, and at deletion vertices it is its negative. Ordinary white sums vanish because their two weights are opposite. Retain only the connected component of the exception.

*Reducing the planar graph.* Among all connected planar bipartite multigraphs with the indicated labels, nonzero edge weights, and balances <a href="#eq:embedding-balances" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:embedding-balances">[eq:embedding-balances]</a>, and with exactly one exceptional white vertex of nonzero sum, choose one minimizing lexicographically the number of vertices and then the number of edges. We do not require this comparison graph to come from a word reduction. Its exceptional vertex has positive degree, so the graph has an edge.

There are no parallel edges. Two parallel edges have weights in the same $L_i^0$ and the same map $\iota_{vi}$ at their black endpoint. Replace them by one edge carrying their sum, or delete both if that sum is zero, and again retain the component of the exception. Additivity preserves every balance and preserves the exceptional nonzero sum. This gives a smaller comparison graph, a contradiction.

Fix a spherical embedding of the minimal graph. We claim that, at each vertex of degree at least two, cyclically consecutive neighbors have distinct labels. If two such neighbors have the same label, they are distinct vertices because parallel edges have been removed. Join them by an auxiliary arc following one side of the two incident edges and the empty sector between those edges, and contract the arc. A sufficiently narrow neighborhood of this two-edge path makes the arc disjoint from the rest of the graph. This construction is local and remains valid when either edge is a bridge.

The contraction merges vertices of the same color and label. Each original edge keeps its weight and its opposite-color endpoint; hence the resulting graph is still bipartite, and every edge weight is nonzero. Parallel edges created by the contraction are allowed in the comparison class. When black vertices are merged, their balances add in the same $K_v^0$, while white balances are unchanged. When white vertices are merged, their balances add in the same $L_i^0$, while every black incidence map and weighted sum is unchanged. If the exception is merged, its sum is increased by zero. Thus the contraction gives a comparison graph with fewer vertices, proving the claim.

<figure id="fig:merge">

<figcaption>The white vertices $p, q$, both labelled $f$, merge to $r$ along the dashed auxiliary arc in the empty sector between their consecutive edges at the black vertex $x$, labelled $v$. The edges of weights $a, b$ remain distinct parallel edges.</figcaption>
</figure>
